Like if there’s a long chapter to read or I need to write out a plan, I’ll put it off until it’s late, and then I’m rushing through it after work.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or paying attention to things?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. At work or in class, it doesn’t take much to throw me off—like a phone ringing, people talking nearby, or even just suddenly thinking about something I need to do at home. Then I realize I’m not really focused on what I was doing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever found yourself unsure of the date, time, or your location?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I know where I am and what day it is. I might lose track of time when I’m busy, but I don’t get confused about the date or where I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're trying to express something?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, sometimes I’ll pause for a second to think of how to say something, but I haven’t noticed a real problem finding words.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble making decisions?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. It’s not like I can’t decide anything, but I get stuck figuring out what to do first. I can usually keep up at work, but since I started evening classes, my desk, my class folder, and my email reminders kind of turn into piles, and then I waste time sorting through it all instead of just starting.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt disoriented or unclear about your surroundings?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I haven’t felt confused about where I am or what’s around me. Sometimes I’m distracted, but not disoriented.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble planning and organizing your daily tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, a bit. I can usually manage at work, but since I started evening classes it’s gotten harder. My desk, my class folder, and my email reminders all kind of build up into piles, and then I spend too much time trying to figure out what I’m supposed to do first instead of just getting started.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down most of the time lately?\n\nPatient: Not really. I mean, I get frustrated or overwhelmed sometimes, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been sad or down most of the time lately.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where your thinking and memory seem sharper than others?\n\nPatient: Some days do seem better than others, but what I notice more is the forgetfulness. I keep missing little things I normally handle, like forgetting to return a call, turn in a registration form, or stop for an errand on my way home. That’s really what made me ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more tired than usual lately?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed being more tired than usual. I mean, I get worn out like anyone, but nothing that stands out as a big change.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble remembering things you've learned in the past?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I haven’t noticed a problem remembering things I learned before. It’s more like… if you mean old information, that seems okay.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble recalling information you've learned when you need it for a task or project?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed that as a big problem, at least not in a clear way. If I learned something before, I can usually pull it up when I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do at a later time?\n\nPatient: Yes, that’s actually been happening. I’ll plan to do something later, and then it just slips my mind—like returning a call, turning in a form, or stopping for an errand on the way home. It’s little things, but it’s been enough that I started feeling like I should ask someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do at a specific time?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s usually small things, but I’ll plan to do something at a certain time and then I just forget. Like returning a call, turning in a registration form, or stopping for an errand on the way home. That’s kind of what finally made me ask for help.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget to do things you had planned to do in the future, like appointments or tasks?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. Like I said, it’s more the everyday little things—returning calls, turning in a registration form, or remembering to stop for an errand on the way home. It’s the kind of stuff I usually handle, but lately I’ll mean to do it and then it just slips my mind. That’s what made me decide to ask for help.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
